Ireland’s labour market steadies but AI hiring is up, finds report

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Indeed’s research finds that the country’s labour market is consistent, while unemployment rates are low. Recruitment platform Indeed has published the findings of its latest ‘Labour Market Snapshot’, which explores how the Irish employment space is performing in the third quarter of the year. The research finds that Ireland’s labour market has steadied over the course of the last few months, with new job postings comparable to an early 2020, pre-pandemic baseline. Ireland’s labour market remains close to full employment, with the unemployment rate remaining at 5pc and the employment rate close to highs. According to Indeed’s report, this is in line with a labour market that has normalised following a period of persistently elevated labour demand post-Covid. “Our latest data suggests Ireland’s labour market has moved into a more normalised phase following the hiring demand we have seen after the pandemic,” said Jack Kennedy, a senior economist at Indeed. The report finds that the majority of sectors tracked sit below their pre-pandemic levels in terms of overall postings on Indeed, with the remaining 41pc sitting at or above baseline. Postings for pharmacy, as well as cleaning and sanitation, are furthest above the February 2020 baseline, while listings for IT infrastructure, operations and support, and childcare are furthest below pre-pandemic levels. Kennedy added, “While overall demand has stabilised, the picture varies by sector, location and occupation, with AI increasingly becoming an important factor of Ireland’s hiring landscape.” Indeed’s research also found that the rate at which AI is being adopted in Ireland is increasing. Almost 15pc of all Irish postings to the jobs site were found to include AI-based terminology in the description, the highest among all European countries tracked by Indeed. Corresponding rates elsewhere included 9.7pc in the UK, 5.6pc in Germany, 5pc in Italy and 3.6pc  in the Netherlands. Further afield, it was 6.7pc in the US. “AI is certainly changing the skills employers are looking for and Ireland is at the forefront of that shift. Around 15pc of Irish job postings mention AI, one of the highest shares internationally,” said Kennedy. “We’re increasingly seeing AI create demand for new skills and roles, and that demand is spreading well beyond traditional tech jobs. The challenge is making sure people, particularly those early into their careers, have the opportunity to develop the skills employers will need as the world of work changes.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Silicon Republic
Maynooth researchers build advanced DNA computer
2026-09-18

Maynooth researchers build advanced DNA computer

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
From left: Janet Adio, Prof Damien Woods and Dr Abeer Eshra. Image: Maynooth University The research points to new possibilities for long-term data storage, energy-efficient computation and molecular systems, according to the team. A team of Maynooth University researchers has developed a “thermodynamically favoured molecular computer” that is thought to be one of the most complex and fastest DNA computers yet created. The work of Prof Damien Woods, Dr Abeer Eshra, Dr Constantine Evans, Janet Adio and Dr Tristan Stérin was published this week (16 September) in the journal Nature. Their research demonstrates a molecular computer made from strands of DNA that can perform multiplication, division and addition. Unlike conventional computers that run on silicon chips, the system is a collection of interacting DNA strands in water without a continuous electricity supply that needs only a small amount of heat to kick-start computations. Woods said: “Silicon-based computers use so much energy; 23pc of Ireland’s electricity goes into computing and data storage. We’ve been blinkered by only seeing one type of computer, but there are other examples around us, including our brain.” According to the team, the research points to new possibilities for long-term data storage, energy-efficient computation and molecular systems that could operate inside cells for applications such as disease detection. The team created the DNA computer by adding a small drop of water and salt to a test tube, together with short pieces of DNA and a longer DNA scaffold. This ‘recipe’ was then heated and cooled, allowing the DNA to perform a computation. “The molecules interact, form a structure and that structure is the answer. One key innovation is that the system naturally finds that answer without needing continuous energy inputs,” said Woods. The university said its DNA computer was “shown to be robust and reusable” and was capable of performing up to 25 different calculations in a row. “A small droplet of liquid contains billions, and sometimes trillions, of DNA strands. These strands interact with one another to produce a result,” said Eshra. “The reaction happens fast in the test tube, but not as fast as silicon – nor is it intended to be. But compared to other DNA computers, ours is the fastest.” Woods leads an EU-funded DNA computing initiative at the university’s Hamilton Institute that explores alternative forms of computing. He said: “This is blue-skies science. We don’t know where the future is going to take us.” The team’s Nature-published research can be viewed here. Bolt, Lucid plan to deploy 25,000 robotaxis in Europe

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Bolt will own and operate the new fleet of autonomous vehicles. Estonian ride-hailing service Bolt has partnered with US carmaker Lucid to deploy at least 25,000 fully autonomous vehicles (AVs) across multiple European cities, marking the latest efforts by businesses to bring robotaxis to the region while regulators attempt to play catch-up with the technology. The partnership will utilise Bolt’s extensive European data along with Lucid’s software-defined vehicle platform to develop an autonomous driving system (ADS)-ready vehicle platform designed for level-four autonomous mobility. The platform will be built on Nvidia’s Hyperion, a production-ready AV reference architecture. Bolt will own and operate the new vehicles, it said. The company hopes to grow its AV fleet to 100,000 by 2035. The Estonian company will work across product development through to commercial operation in its new partnership, helping define vehicle requirements as well as software, safety and rider-experience parameters. It will also build the fleet infrastructure, operating systems and city partnerships needed to deploy the AVs at scale. “Autonomous driving in Europe requires data, software, vehicles and operations to work as one system built for European roads and regulation,” said Markus Villig, the founder and CEO of Bolt. “Lucid brings a world-class platform, and together, we will co-design the vehicle and software based on our data and more than a decade of operating experience across more than 850 cities.” Robotaxis are a common sight across major US and Chinese cities, but expanding operations to older European cities with often rainy weather has been a challenge for global AV leaders. Last month, Alphabet-owned Waymo announced it would introduce driverless robotaxis on German roads by the end of next year, while Uber and Pony AI said they would deploy more than 2,000 robotaxis across Europe. Uber launched its first European robotaxi service in Croatia late last month in partnership with Pony and Croatian mobility company Verne, while its London deployment began taking rides earlier this month. Visit Original Website

Kennedy Jr. announced that the government will investigate “chemtrails” — a conspiracy theory held by people who would have you believe that the airplanes of the world are secretly seeding our skies with chemicals to change the weather, or maybe biological agents to keep the masses under control. Kennedy made this avowal while giving a keynote speech at none other than the Children’s Health Defense, an anti-vaccine advocacy nonprofit that he helped create (a newsworthy item in itself). Living up to the occasion, his speech was replete with crank ramblings and unproven health claims, but one aside stood out from all the others. “We’re also looking at chemtrails at the impact of emissions,” RFK wheezed, to a round of applause. He finishes his sentence. “Emissions from, um, aircraft, and those potential impacts on health.” RFK Jr: "We are also looking at chemtrails" pic.twitter.com/9vjjdnXXjO It’s a fully mask-off moment for the Kennedy scion, if he had any more masks left to remove. His appearance at the CHD shows a level of brazenness in his anti-vaccine beliefs that he hasn’t been quite as overt about since taking up his role as Trump’s health secretary. Chemtrails are the cherry on top of all the madness. The term is from a long-held conspiracy theory that the trails that airplanes drag across the sky actually contain nefarious chemicals that’re used for everything from population control to, most commonly, weather modification (delusional beliefs vary). That RFK is now treating the idea of chemtrails seriously shows how far off the conspiracy deep end he’s gone — at least anti-vaxxers pretend their beliefs have basis in science — or perhaps how emboldened he feels to show his true colors. He previously vowed to look into chemtrails last year; about a month later, the Environmental Protection Agency formally debunked the conspiracy theory. The reality is planes’ visual residue are contrails, which are streaks of condensation created by jet engines from the interaction between the aircraft’s hot exhaust — which is mostly just carbon dioxide and water vapor — and the cold atmosphere. It’s the same reason that you see your breath on a cold day. There’s nothing to investigate, and even if there was, it would make no sense for a figure like RFK to do it, since the conspiracy theory is that the government is perpetrating it and covering it up. Meanwhile, in the real world, RFK’s stewardship of the US’s health agencies has overseen the nation’s worst measles outbreak in over three decades, which was almost certainly caused, at least in part, by the anti-vaccine rhetoric that he and other right wing figures have pushed. Pennsylvania, the epicenter of the outbreak, has recorded over 3,300 cases, with a scandal brewing on how the RFK-led Centers for Disease Control and Prevention is refusing to count some measles deaths reported by Pennsylvania health officials. Yet Kennedy is still concerned with all this chemtrails nonsense — his head, in an almost literal sense, in the clouds. So Many Women Are Miscarrying in Detention Centers That ICE Has Lost Count

Visit Original Website

Unfortunately, a record number of pregnant women have been swept up and sent to such facilities — and it’s hard to imagine a worse place to be during the prenatal period. Horrifying new reporting by the Guardian took a hard look at the state of health care for pregnant women and teenagers held in ICE custody. During the first nine months of Trump’s return to the White House, the agency recorded 18 miscarriages from women held in its custody, an already appalling statistic. Yet as the Guardian found, ICE’s miscarriage records only go up to October of 2025, and any subsequent loss of pregnancy has been lost in the shuffle. That blatant disregard for critical health information underscores the nightmarish conditions pregnant women face in federal detention facilities. One woman, an asylum seeker from Nicaragua named Anabell, relayed her experience to the Guardian. After being detained at the end of a routine government check-in, Anabell says she was given two pregnancy tests, both of which came back positive. That was it, however — the standard blood test and dosage of prenatal vitamins were nowhere to be found, and the amount of protein she was given hardly changed. A month into her detention, Anabell told the Guardian she started to bleed. “I told them I need to see a doctor,” she said, though ICE officers were unmoved. After spending another day in the ICE facility, Anabell’s fellow detainees finally convinced guards to bring her to an emergency room, where she was shackled to a bed and monitored by three ICE agents. Through an interpreter, Anabell learned that she had lost the pregnancy. She has continued to face health complications including bleeding, vomiting, rashes, as well as hair and weight loss, for which she hasn’t received any additional care. “I don’t know what is going to happen to me,” she told the paper. As Sarah Decker, a senior attorney at the Robert and Ethel Kennedy Human Rights Center told the Guardian, it’s unlikely ICE is truly unaware of the amount of pregnant women in the department’s custody, or the horrible health conditions they face. “Many of the pregnant women that we’ve met at [the South Louisiana processing center in] Basile have told us that they disclosed their pregnancy to ICE officers, that they had to advocate for access to prenatal care and access to a gynecologist, that they needed emergency care for complications, Decker said. “So ICE is clearly aware and I think their desire to kind of obfuscate the access that people have to this data is intended to cover up the level of life-threatening medical neglect that’s happening in these facilities.” More on ICE: ICE Reveals Horrific Plan to Outfit Agents With Electrocution Torture Gloves

What this kind of hobby — and unrelenting exposure to AI slop — could do to a developing mind remains a debated subject among experts. But the early consensus is troubling. And as Maxima Zuckerberg’s father knows all too well, ensuring that underage users aren’t exposed to this type of content is far from easy. The Majority of Americans Are Now Terrified of AI
2026-09-18

The Majority of Americans Are Now Terrified of AI

Futurism | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
The majority of Americans are harboring some apocalyptic fears about AI. According to a new Politico poll that surveyed over 2,000 adults, about two thirds of the American public believes there’s now at least a “moderate” chance that advanced AI could destroy humanity. Intriguingly, the prevalence of this view extends across political lines, with only slight variation. Americans who voted for Kamala Harris in the 2024 presidential election were 70 percent likely to view AI as at a moderate or higher existential risk, compared to 60 percent to Trump voters. There’s also a large contingent who’re quite fatalistic about the tech. A full 17 percent of Americans overall are “almost certain” that AI could wipe us all out, with an even split between Harris and Trump voters. Meanwhile, less than a quarter — 22 percent — said that AI posed a very small to no risk. The poll, conducted this September, is an intriguing barometer of public sentiment as AI leaders have made alarming claims about the risks posed by their systems’ capabilities. An Anthropic researcher stirred the pot last week when he quit the company and accused it of “gambling with our lives,” claiming that the people building the tech “earnestly believe that it could kill us all.” As alarm brewed, Anthropic’s Dario Amodei penned a lengthy essay calling on the US’s top AI companies to cooperate on slowing down AI development, earning the endorsements of Elon Musk and OpenAI CEO Sam Altman. There’s some real skepticism over the sincerity of the AI industry’s safety claims, but they appear to be a loud minority. Only 26 percent said they thought AI leaders were warning of AI’s existential risks as a marketing stunt, per the poll’s findings. By and large, the public is buying what AI leaders are saying. A hefty 48 percent of Americans agreed that AI is now advanced enough that we should outright pause development. There was more divergence here across party lines, though, with only 44 percent of Trump voters holding this view, compared to 58 percent of Harris voters. Conversely, 28 percent of Harris voters said we should continue building powerful AIs because of their potential benefits, compared to 40 percent of the president’s backers. Americans largely agree that the threat is real, but are split on how to tackle it. More on AI: Fear of AI Destroying Humanity Cause Certain Stocks to Skyrocket

India forces caller-ID apps to feed spam reports to telcos
2026-09-19

India forces caller-ID apps to feed spam reports to telcos

TechCrunch | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt India has extended its anti-spam regime to require caller-ID and call-management apps to share users’ spam reports with telecom operators, prompting spam-blocking app maker Truecaller to call the ruling anti-competitive. On Friday, the Telecom Regulatory Authority of India (TRAI), the country’s telecom regulator, amended rules governing commercial communications, making it mandatory for call-ID and call-management apps that let users flag calls as spam or junk to send those reports to a blockchain-based platform maintained by telecom operators. The platform tracks commercial communications and enforces anti-spam rules. The change, TRAI said, is intended to broaden the pool of spam reports available for action against spammers, effectively connecting reports collected by apps with the telecom industry’s enforcement infrastructure. However, Truecaller told TechCrunch that it sees this requirement as a “one-way exchange” that is “anti-competitive,” arguing that it transfers commercially valuable data from call-management apps like itself to telecom operators. India is Truecaller’s largest market, accounting for well over 350 million of its more than 500 million monthly active users globally. The Stockholm-based company uses community reports alongside automated detection and other signals to identify and block spam calls. The rules come as India grapples with spam and fraudulent calls at enormous scale. In its report in February, Truecaller said its users in the country encountered around 42 billion spam calls in 2025, including calls that were blocked, labeled, or ignored. The company also stated that it blocked nearly 12 billion spam calls during the year. It is not the first time Truecaller and the Indian regulator have been at odds over how spam calls should be handled. The Swedish company previously objected to restrictions preventing call-management apps from automatically labeling calls from certain government-designated number ranges as spam. It argued that the exemption could allow unwanted calls to escape its filters. However, Friday’s amendments retain that restriction and have barred call-management apps from blanket blocking, filtering, or spam-tagging calls from designated number series used for promotional, service, and transactional communications. Individual users can still choose to block such calls on their own devices, the regulator said. “While our data and user sentiment clearly show that spam has skyrocketed due to this free pass to spammers, we have been compliant with this since late last year,” A Truecaller spokesperson said. Sumeysh Srivastava, a partner at New Delhi-based consulting firm The Quantum Hub, who leads its telecom-regulation policy work, said the latest change bridges two distinct layers: telecom operators provide the underlying network and run the blockchain-based anti-spam system, while caller-ID apps operate on top of the network to identify and filter calls. That raises technical and jurisdictional questions, Srivastava told TechCrunch, including what reporting standards apps will have to follow and how the requirement will be enforced against companies that are not themselves telecom operators. A March draft proposed (PDF) using India’s IT laws to enforce the requirement. However, Srivastava pointed out that the new announcement did not say whether that enforcement mechanism was retained in the final rules. It is also unclear how much information the apps will actually have to provide under the updated regulation. Kazim Rizvi, founding director of New Delhi-based policy think tank The Dialogue, told TechCrunch that requiring an app to transmit a specific spam report made by a user is materially different from requiring it to share the broader datasets, reputation signals, or analytical systems it uses to identify suspicious calls. The rules will need clarity on what information must be transmitted, how users are notified or asked for consent, and how that data can subsequently be retained and used, Rizvi said. TRAI did not respond to TechCrunch’s questions about what information apps would be required to share and whether the rule would also apply to spam-reporting features built into smartphone operating systems and dialers such as Android and iOS. The amendments also address the growing use of software and AI voice agents to make calls. Calls made automatically, without a person directly dialing the number, will now fall under TRAI’s application-to-person (A2P) framework. That includes robocalls and calls using prerecorded or artificial voices. Companies using such systems will have to declare their use and the phone numbers involved to their telecom operators in advance. Undeclared A2P calls will be treated as spam, TRAI said. The key test, Srivastava said, is how a call is initiated, rather than simply whether it uses an AI-generated voice, leaving some uncertainty around AI-assisted calls that involve human initiation. Satya N. Gupta, a former additional secretary at TRAI, told TechCrunch that the new rules do not restrict businesses from using AI or other automated calling technologies, but instead require them to disclose their use to telecom operators. Telecom operators will also be allowed to levy a termination charge of up to 5 paise (about 0.052 cents) per minute on A2P calls. However, calls made using certain designated number ranges will be exempt. Rizvi told TechCrunch that the new definition could also cover calls made using software even when a person is still involved, such as calls from contact centers and click-to-call services. “Without that distinction, the A2P category risks becoming broader than the regulatory harm it is intended to address,” he said. (I was not invited to speak with it, and frankly, I cannot imagine why Particle6 didn’t want to schedule that meeting.) In one particularly odd interview with Piers Morgan and actor Tom Conti, which was recorded and posted online, Norwood seems to malfunction and abruptly begin speaking Chinese. When discussing the movie that Norwood is promoting, Conti asks if the other actors are also AI-generated. Norwood responds by saying that human writers, editors, and directors were involved. “You didn’t really understand the question,” Conti replies. “Are the other actors on the screen with you real actors, or are they computer-generated images? A startup that builds other startups raised $100M and is all-in on physical AI

Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Four years ago, a startup lab launched that wasn’t quite an incubator, accelerator program, or venture firm. UP.Labs, as it was called then, built startups designed to solve problems for corporate customers such as Alaska Airlines and Porsche, as well as for the outside world. The firm still has the same mission — albeit with a critical addition to its approach, a new name, and a $100 million investment from Silversmith Capital Partners. Vantora, as the firm is now called, continues to work with its corporate customers, including some new ones in industrial manufacturing that it declined to name, and in the oil and gas sector. But now it’s more focused on building startups solely for its corporate customers and not for the broader market. Founder and CEO John Kuolt told TechCrunch that Vantora is moving toward a “proprietary M&A pipeline.” This means Vantora will still build startups for its corporate partners, which invest in the ventures and serve as their first customers. But those corporate partners now have the option to fold the startups into their core businesses — and essentially keep them to themselves. That shift has influenced Vantora’s increased focus on physical AI startups, according to Kuolt. In the past, Vantora would end up spiking ideas that were strategic to its corporate partners, but too sensitive to bring to the outside world. “We were missing on the biggest value problems, which had the biggest upside because of that,” Kuolt said in a recent interview. “Imagine you’re a Fortune 100 industrial company and you need to retrofit all of your hardware and machines for autonomy. You need to own that, it needs to be sovereign, and you can’t rely on a third party to go do that for you. You need to own that intelligence layer. They’re never going to let us go sell that to their competitors.” This change has allowed Vantora to “unlock big physical AI use cases,” according to Kuolt, including with its existing customers. For example, the firm came up with an idea to use AI to advance the business of its partner J.B. Hunt. “They said there is no way you can take this out to the world, and so we passed on it,” he said, adding that this proprietary model now allows Vantora to pursue it. The firm launched in 2022 with Porsche as its first corporate partner. Since then, Vantora has launched several startups for Porsche and struck deals with Alaska Airlines, J.B. Hunt, Wabash, and TDG, the parent of Ashley Furniture. In its early days, UP.Labs was tied — although never financially — to venture firm Up.Partners. While Vantora still shares office space with the California-based VC, it is its own entity, Kuolt explained, noting that the $100 million from Silversmith is the company’s first outside investment. The near-miss comes as the U.S. military races to integrate AI to accelerate decision-making and maintain its edge over China. The Pentagon has described AI as delivering a significant advantage in speeding up its kill chain so commanders can respond in the right time. But the same speed that makes AI attractive may also allow hallucinations with insufficient human oversight. “It’s important for service members to understand the uncertainty inherent to LLMs,” said Jake Steckler, research scholar at GovAI and veteran U.S. Army officer, in a written response to TechCrunch. “But it’s especially critical for any decisions that could lead to use of force, like targeting, intelligence analysis, or operational planning. How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice
2026-09-18

How a New Trump Administration Rule Could Restrict Access to Green Cards—and Confront Immigrants With a Tough Choice

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump walks on his way to attend a dinner in the Rose Garden of the White House in Washington, D.C. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump’s Administration is rolling out a new rule that experts predict will make it more difficult for immigrants to get green cards if they access public assistance programs, such as Medicaid and food stamps—and could deter those hoping to obtain the status from enrolling in public benefits. The rule, which goes into effect on Friday, updates a policy dating back to the 19th century that the U.S. has long used to prohibit immigrants who are believed to be likely to become a “public charge” from getting green cards. Trump’s Department of Homeland Security (DHS) has argued that the change “is restoring the basic principle that immigrants must be able to support themselves,” adding that it is “reaffirming the requirement of self-reliance, protecting public resources, and ending policies that encouraged dependency on hard-working American taxpayers.” The rule marks the latest move in Trump’s efforts to sharply restrict both illegal and legal immigration. The President enacted a similar change during his first Administration, but the effort was subjected to court challenges and ultimately rescinded by his successor, Joe Biden. The new rule has already been challenged in court by a coalition of more than a dozen states, including New York and California. It’s unclear at this point how the ongoing litigation will affect the rollout of the rule. Here’s what to know about the new rule and how it could affect immigrants. With its new policy, the Trump Administration is implementing a change to what’s known as the “public charge” rule, a policy first established under the Immigration Act of 1882. Since then, one factor that the U.S. government has considered when reviewing green card applications is whether the applicant is likely to become dependent on government assistance. Historically, officials reviewing these types of applications have only taken into account cash benefit programs—for instance, Supplemental Security Income from Social Security—as “public benefits” that could lead to an immigrant being deemed a “public charge” and having their green card application denied. They have usually not deemed non-cash public benefits, such as Medicaid and food stamps, as programs that would disqualify an immigrant from obtaining permanent resident status. During his first term in the White House, Trump tried to expand the programs that could result in an immigrant being deemed a “public charge,” but a federal judge ruled that the policy had to be vacated, saying it had “numerous unexplained flaws” that made it “arbitrary and capricious.” When former President Joe Biden was in office, he officially rescinded Trump’s rule and stipulated that non-cash benefits would not result in an immigrant being considered a “public charge.” In July, the Trump Administration revealed that it was revoking the Biden-era policy and was rolling out a change similar to the one that faced legal challeges during Trump’s first term. Under the new rule, officials reviewing green card applications are able to consider a broader scope of public benefit programs. The Trump Administration has noted that the new “public charge” rule does not apply to some immigrants—for instance, refugees. The guidance, though, is vague—it doesn’t include a full list of the benefits that can be taken into consideration, saying only that immigration officers will be “using good judgment and discretion” and will be “making individualized, fact-specific public charge inadmissibility determinations, based on a totality of the alien’s circumstances.” “Now, the officers who are reviewing and adjudicating these cases have much more discretion in how they make that determination—whether they believe the person is likely to become a public charge,” says Melissa Shepard, the legal services director at the Immigrant Defenders Law Center. “The difference now is that it’s a much broader analysis, whereas before it was a little more limited.” Adriana Cadena, the executive director of the Protecting Immigrant Families Coalition, says she worries that, because the new policy is so broad, it “opens the door for abuses by immigration officials” during the green card application process. “It’s going from having guidance and clarity and understanding to opening the door to any kinds of programs,” she says. Immigration experts say that the new policy could result in more green card applications being rejected, as well as cause many immigrants to delay seeking permanent resident status. Charles Kuck, an immigration lawyer and former national president of the American immigration Lawyers Association, says that he expects there will be “massive numbers of denials” of green card applications as this new policy is rolled out. He adds that he and his colleagues have heard from some of their clients that they plan to wait until after Trump leaves the White House before filing their green card applications. People who do not have permanent resident status in the U.S. are not eligible for many public assistance programs, but experts also warn that the new rule could result in immigrants disenrolling from public benefit programs that they or their children are eligible for because they are afraid of potentially harming their chances of obtaining a green card in the future. Experts are additionally concerned that the Trump Administration’s new rule opens the door to immigration officials taking into account whether a green card applicant’s family member used a public benefit. The new rule stipulates that “if there is evidence in the record the alien’s family members whom the alien is legally obligated to support receive means-tested public benefits based on the alien’s income falling below the designated threshold, DHS would consider that fact.” Kuck says he fears that this could lead to a situation where, for example, if a green card applicant’s child who is a U.S. citizen receives a public benefit, such as a free school lunch program, that could lead to their parent’s application being denied. According to Shepard, it’s not yet clear how exactly the new policy will be applied in practice because it is so broad and gives immigration officers “so much discretion.” But she says she believes the change will cause people to be “a lot more cautious” and potentially fearful to access public assistance programs. Cadena says the new rule is “forcing families to choose between either being able to adjust their status in the future or be able to have access to essential programs like food and child care.” “In the current environment that we are in, where immigrant family safety is constantly being threatened, immigrant families … want to be able to keep their families together,” Cadena says. “And if keeping their child enrolled in an important program like Medicaid is going to threaten that, then they would more than likely disenroll their children from these programs.” DHS has previously estimated that the rollout of the “public charge” change could lead to more than 950,000 people disenrolling from or choosing not to enroll in public assistance programs. The change is the latest action the Trump Administration has taken to restrict immigration, including ramping up efforts to limit several legal pathways to immigration and proposing raising the fees associated with applying for citizenship. “This is just one small factor or small piece of the entire immigration system, I think, that is under attack,” Shepard says.

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured

Why the Outcome of Russia's First Parliamentary Elections in Years Is All But Assured

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In this pool photograph distributed by the Russian state agency Sputnik, Russia's President Vladimir Putin gives a speech during a ceremony to present state awards in the defence industry sector at the Kremlin in Moscow on September 18, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Russia is just one day into three-day elections that will determine the makeup of the lower house of the country’s parliament, the State Duma. But the outcome of the vote has been all but guaranteed since before the first ballots were cast. The elections, the first parliamentary vote the country has held since its invasion of Ukraine in 2022, are widely expected to result in the Kremlin-aligned United Russia party maintaining its overwhelming dominance in the Duma. The party currently holds 310 of the 450 seats in the chamber, giving it a supermajority and the power to make major legislative decisions without the input of other parties. It needs to keep at least 300 seats to retain that supermajority. Four other political parties are also expected to be represented in the Duma following the election, all of which generally vote in line with United Russia and support the war in Ukraine. They are the Communist Party of the Russian Federation, the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia, the A Just Russia party, and the New People party. Democracy watchdogs and foreign governments have denounced Russia’s elections as being neither free nor fair. Russian officials have rejected such claims in the past, saying that the country carries out its voting process in line with the law. But experts tell TIME that the Russian government exerts its influence in ways that all but predetermine the results of elections and ensure that President Vladimir Putin and United Russia maintain control. And this year’s vote, says David Szakonyi, an expert on Russia and election fraud who is professor at George Washington University, is “occurring in the most repressive political environment in Russia's post-Soviet history.” Here's what experts have to say about how the Kremlin ensures elections result in its desired outcome—and why it continues to hold them. The Kremlin uses multiple levers to influence elections on the government level, at the polls, and in daily civilian life, according to Szakonyi. “Without a doubt, the most powerful tool” in the Kremlin’s arsenal, Szakonyi says, is limiting which candidates appear on the ballot in the first place. In the case of the ongoing parliamentary vote, the one anti-war party that was registered by the Central Election Commission, the Yabloko party, was prohibited from participating in the election by Russia’s Supreme Court. The high court ordered that the party be removed from the ballot last month in response to a lawsuit from the pro-Kremlin Rodina party that accused the Yabloko party, which has garnered support from young Russians and those opposed to the war in Ukraine, of copyright infringement, receiving foreign funding, and extremism. Szakoni says that the Kremlin has the ability to “harness the judicial system to pass rulings” such as the Supreme Court’s decision against the Yabloko party, which it uses as a “legal veneer” that shields the influence of Putin’s government over the ballot. This screening of anti-war and other opposition parties, he says, comes months in advance of elections, creating a list of candidates that includes little or no meaningful opposition at all. The Kremlin has also labeled opposition figures who oppose the war as “foreign agents,” a designation that prevents them from holding public office. In other instances, opposition leaders and activists such as Alexei Navalny have been prosecuted and imprisoned. Still others have fled the country. Experts tell TIME that the Russian government has also exercised its influence more directly on vote counts to achieve its desired election results. “Historically,” Szakonyi says, Russian electoral officials have “intervened regularly to deliver votes to the ruling party and take them away from competitors.” In the past, this was done via “crude ballot box stuffing,” he says; now, he asserts it is also performed by altering the results of online votes. Online voting is used in more than a third of Russia’s regions, which Szakonyi asserts puts more power in the government’s hands since it eliminates the physical tally represented by paper ballots. “The way that the electronic votes are being counted is almost completely a black box for the rest of society,” he says. Regina Smyth, an expert on Russia and authoritarian regimes who is a professor at Indiana University, says that “falsification,” or the practice of altering the number of votes that are counted, occurs at precinct election commissions, of which there are over 90,000, all across Russia, that she says are “mostly staffed by loyalists, and they will simply report false numbers.” There is also fraud, Smyth alleges, in “mobile voting,” when officials deliver and pick up ballots from people in their homes who are, for example, disabled or ill and unable to vote otherwise. On a deeper level, an “implicit threat” stemming from the Russian government sways countless votes each election cycle, Szakonyi contends. “Many Russian voters do not have the autonomy to cast an independent vote. They work for the state,” he says. He argues that many university students, healthcare workers, and teachers, whose livelihoods depend on state-run organizations, belong to a “block of captive, vulnerable voters” who fear prosecution by the Kremlin more than they value the influence of their ballot. Voting is also taking place this year in Russian-controlled areas of Ukraine: in Crimea, which Russia annexed in 2014, and in the Luhansk, Donetsk, Kherson, and Zaporizhzhia regions. “In the occupied territories of Ukraine, where the elections are being held in these illegally occupied territories, soldiers, heavily armed soldiers, travel from house to house coercing people to vote,” Smyth tells TIME. On top of the various methods the Kremlin allegedly uses to achieve the voting results it wants, there are slim to no oversight mechanisms in place to document election interference in the country. Golos, which was the only independent election watchdog within Russia, shuttered in 2025 following years of governmental pressure after its chairman was found guilty of working with an "undesirable organization" and imprisoned in a penal colony. Szakonyi describes Golos’ loss as the “biggest blow toward any type of accountability” for the Kremlin, saying it was “the biggest tool that civil society had to identify, uncover, and publicize the fraud that was basically happening in every single electoral cycle.” Russia has also refused to invite international election monitors from the Organization for Security ​and Co-operation in Europe (OSCE), the world’s largest regional security organization, to observe this year’s elections. Maria Telalian, director of the OSCE’s election-monitoring office, the Office for Democratic Institutions and Human Rights, said in a statement last month that the decision not to invite any OSCE monitors was “deeply regrettable, particularly as it continues a pattern of not implementing the commitments that every OSCE participating State has undertaken towards its own citizens and the international community.” The ongoing vote marks the third nationwide election in Russia to take place without OSCE observation. Russia previously imposed severe limits on OSCE election observation in 2021 that the organization said made monitoring that year’s parliamentary elections in the country impossible. It did not invite the group’s monitors to the 2024 presidential election at all. Despite the results of the parliamentary elections being all but determined, they carry a symbolic weight crucial to Putin’s legitimacy in the public eye and his desire to continue the war against Ukraine, experts tell TIME. “There's lots of reasons to have elections. First and foremost, the Russian population expects elections,” Szakonyi says. “Putin's legitimacy depends on his ability to win election votes to the presidency and have his ruling party win on a regular interval, and demonstrate that they're popular and that their policies are in demand from the citizenry.” During an address in which Putin urged Russians to vote days before the elections began, the leader said that “your choice and resolve will show once again that millions of people stand behind our fighters” and that “participating in the elections is our shared contribution to Russia’s victory.” “This is a way for him to theoretically show that the Russian population is united behind his war and wants to continue fighting, and that Russia has had this referendum and will continue to push harder,” Szakonyi says.

Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage
2026-09-18

Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Has Nothing New to Say About Female Rage

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings This article discusses the full season of Netflix’s Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story is about female rage and repression. That isn’t my personal interpretation of the latest installment of Ian Brennan and Ryan Murphy’s Netflix true-crime anthology. It’s the talking point that the show’s makers have been leading with, in publicity materials and in multiple interviews. These are fine themes for the first season of Monster with a female subject, even if they’re not exactly unique within a post-Big-Little-Lies, #MeToo-informed TV landscape where so many dramas aimed at women double as amplifiers for gendered frustrations. The well of misogyny isn’t in danger of running dry anytime soon. But if we must keep diving into it, each descent should at least leave us with some new insight or nuance. Lizzie Borden, now streaming, fails to clear even that bar. Perhaps the least historically accurate Monster yet—and that’s saying something—the season uses the infamous tale of a Fall River, Mass. woman who probably hacked her wealthy father and stepmother to death with an axe in 1892 as a conduit to just about every plague on womankind you can imagine, past, present, and perennial. Rape. Domestic violence. Pregnancy and childbirth. Housework. Menstrual maladies. Pressure to marry. The premium put on physical beauty. The perils of sex work. Confinement in the home and subjugation to male relatives. By tying together Lizzie’s story with the legends of supposed forerunner Elizabeth Báthory and descendent Aileen Wuornos, Brennan, who wrote every episode, suggests a simplistic kinship between all female killers. Rebecca Hall, left, and Ella Beatty in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix There’s no use fretting over the extremely purposeful anachronisms and inaccuracies in this retelling, which plays as fast and loose with the Borden family as that classic nursery rhyme. With 26-year-old Ella Beatty starring as a dreamy, blank-screen Lizzie, who ages from 32 to her 60s by the end of the season, and Charlie Hunnam and Rebecca Hall cast as parents who were really a couple of decades the actors’ seniors, everyone is much younger and prettier than their real-life counterparts. And more shocking, in some ways, than the hyperspeed double murder reenactment that opens the premiere, is what immediately follows it: a flashback to four months earlier, when Lizzie scares off a suitor by passing a bucket of menstrual blood on the white sofa where they’re sitting. As it turns out, what the fictionalized Bordens refer to as her “lady time” had some relevance to the actual case. But Lizzie Borden is such a massive remix, mingling facts with half-truths and rumors and fabrications, it barely matters which detail is which. Because its source material is not especially extensive—one double homicide, as opposed to the lengthy and lurid sagas of most past Monster subjects—the show is forced to stretch. The series is padded with Fall River horror scenes that either never happened (the oyster poisoning that leads to so much operatic barfing) or didn’t transpire remotely the way they’re shown (the murder of Bertha Manchester, played by Linda Reiter). Brennan also adds queer love to Lizzie’s life, in the form of the family’s maid, Bridget Sullivan, who they really did call Maggie, after a former servant. He is far from the first to investigate this angle; the 2018 film Lizzie, to take just one example, is also built around a romance, between Chloë Sevigny’s Lizzie and Kristen Stewart’s Bridget. Monster is, however, the first dramatization to make such imaginative use of the character. Phantom Thread breakout Vicky Krieps, who played ‘Bitch of Buchenwald’ Ilse Koch in Monster’s Ed Gein season, is a conspicuously Germanic Irish maid, with an ahistorical shag haircut dyed a series of shades reminiscent of, by turns, Wuornos and Ziggy Stardust. She has picked up skills in everything from cooking and cleaning to pharmacology in the course of her global travels. It’s no mystery why the awkward, sheltered Lizzie, who craves a bigger life, is enchanted by this glamorous, androgynous outsider. Maggie is easily the best part of the season, thanks in no small part to Krieps’ at once humane and otherworldly performance. Charlie Hunnam and Vicky Krieps in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Suzanne Tenner—Netflix She’s also the unstuck-in-time character who first connects Lizzie to murderesses from other eras. As Maggie reads from a French book about Báthory, a 16th century Hungarian noblewoman said to have killed hundreds of young women and bathed in their blood in pursuit of eternal youth, we see Krieps—with hair and makeup straight out of the goth club—as the so-called Blood Countess. The Runaways’ anthem of teen-girl rebellion “Cherry Bomb” blares. And Lizzie is electrified by the story. She feels a kinship with Báthory. Never mind that killing your cruel parents to free yourself and massacring a village’s worth of peasant girls because La Mer has yet to be invented are about as different as murder motives get. The fact that each woman is acting in response to some form of sexism is, I guess, supposed to bridge that gulf. It takes almost half the season for the third member of the deadly trio, played by Murphyverse MVP Sarah Paulson, to show up. Often framed as America’s first female serial killer, Wuornos, a Florida sex worker who killed seven johns between 1989 and 1990, is, in this telling, as inspired by Lizzie as Lizzie is by Báthory. Her presence, and her unsuccessful attempt to claim in court that she acted in self-defense against rape, allows the show to take on—and force us to watch—sexual violence. I’m not sure it needed to be shoehorned into the rare true-crime drama where it doesn’t have an obvious place. Brennan also connects Aileen’s well-documented queerness with the speculative relationship between Lizzie and Maggie. On a pilgrimage to the Borden home, now a bed and breakfast, Aileen and her girlfriend (Jessie Lee) have public sex. A fantasy sequence has the two couples dancing side-by-side in Lizzie’s sacred garden shed. But it’s never clear what Monster is trying to say about lesbians and murder, and this is a case where ambiguity leaves space to insert some pretty dark assumptions. The outcomes for each of the three female killers are as different as their motivations. Lizzie is acquitted and buys a mansion with her inheritance, presumably because sexist men can’t conceive of a woman committing such physical murders—and because she learns to correctly perform femininity, crying and fainting at appropriate moments, during the trial. Despite wrenching testimony, coarse, unfeminine Aileen is convicted and executed. This might lead you to conclude that privilege and falling in line with patriarchal expectations are what it takes for a woman to get away with murder. But then there’s the rich, powerful, and apparently quite appearance-obsessed Báthory. Our last glimpse of the countess is as a rotting corpse, bricked up in her castle for her sins. So what is really being said here? Sarah Paulson, left, and Jessie Lee in Monster: The Lizzie Borden Story Netflix Not much, as far as I can tell. Lizzie Borden certainly makes a strong case that women have always been repressed, that rage is the obvious product of that repression, and that liberation is the only antidote. (Of course we get a scene in the finale where Lizzie celebrates suffrage.) But if you didn’t know that, it’s probably also news to you that water is wet and the pope is Catholic. The show’s obsession with women’s bodies as the sites of unending horrors reminds me of House of the Dragon’s repeated depictions of traumatic childbirth. It’s as though viewers are expected to go in completely ignorant of basic hardships afflicting half the human population and must see them reproduced over and over again in gory detail to be enlightened, much less empathetic. And in that case, I suppose it’s also necessary to render interchangeable three notorious women defined by actions rooted in a near-universal form of rage. But theirs are old stories, already told and retold in every conceivable media, and often, effectively, by women. Wuornos was, of course, most famously portrayed by Charlize Theron, in writer-director Patty Jenkins’ career-making, Oscar-winning 2003 film also titled Monster. Isabelle Huppert stars as Báthory in The Blood Countess, a new feature by German experimental cinema legend Ulrike Ottinger, which is earning praise on the festival circuit for its strangeness and sense of humor. Meanwhile, on TV, creator Liz Meriwether just gave us Furious—another story of female rage, female repression, and yes, even a (fictional) female serial killer. While Krieps is mesmerizing in Monster and frequent Murphy production designer Matthew Flood Ferguson has outdone himself with lavishly suffocating Gilded Age interiors, Furious is the superior show because it takes “being a woman is hard” as the given it is rather than as the endpoint of many repetitive episodes. It has dark, lived-in comedy and real stakes, posing the question of what can realistically be done about systemic misogyny and the anger over it that stains women’s lives. “All your books are written by men,” Maggie scolds Lizzie in Monster’s season premiere. When you consider how heavily male the creative team behind Lizzie Borden is (executive producer Max Winkler directed six episodes and Sarah Adina Smith, two), this kind of has to be a self-aware line. But whether it’s intended as a wry apology or a small joke, I couldn’t tell you.

Trump Announces Ban of CNN, Politico and MS Now From White House

Trump Announces Ban of CNN, Politico and MS Now From White House

Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump announced on Friday he was banning CNN, MS NOW, and Politico from the White House, setting up a new test of the First Amendment’s protection of a free press as his Administration moves to punish news organizations whose coverage he dislikes. In a post on Truth Social, Trump accused the three outlets of repeatedly publishing “FAKE NEWS” and warned that other news organizations could face similar restrictions. “Effective immediately, I am banning Fake News CNN, MSNOW... and Politico... from the White House as a result of their constant ‘reporting’ FAKE NEWS!” Trump wrote. “Media Outlets shouldn’t be able to constantly write or report FICTION and LIES when they’re covering the President of the United States, the Trump Administration, or the United States of America.” Taking questions from reporters in the Oval Office later, Trump said there were no particular stories that prompted the decision. “It's really just cumulative stories over the last few years, you get sick of it,” he said. “They purposely write negative news, and they do that because they want to try and diminish the Republicans and a Republican administration.” It was not immediately clear whether the White House would enforce the prohibition. Despite Trump’s announcement that the ban was effective immediately, CNN reported that its team remained at the White House and continued to cover the Administration on Friday afternoon. No evictions had been observed in the press briefing room as of 5 p.m., although the mood among reporters there was tense. “CNN stands fully behind our White House team and their fair and accurate reporting,” the network said in a statement. “We have a right under the U.S. Constitution to do that reporting without hindrance or interference from the government. Should the ban which President Trump threatened go ahead, it would be an illegal assault on that fundamental and constitutionally-protected right.” When asked by a reporter what exactly the ban would entail, Trump said, "I don't want them in my office. I don't want them here. I would say the ban would go as far as you can." The White House Press Office did not respond to questions about the ban. Last year, Trump barred reporters from The Associated Press from the Oval Office, Air Force One, and other events with limited space after the news organization declined to update their widely used stylebook to adopt his preferred name, “Gulf of America,” for the body of water long known as the Gulf of Mexico. The Associated Press sued, and the case remains ongoing. The uncertainty could set up another legal confrontation over the boundaries of presidential control over access to the White House and the First Amendment’s protection of freedom of the press. The First Amendment protects freedom of the press, a principle intended to allow news organizations to scrutinize those in power without government retaliation for their reporting. Asked about the likelihood of his latest media ban being struck down in court, Trump responded, “I think it's good to point it out whether it survives or doesn't.” He added, “I don't think that a court should allow fake news to be written day after day after day.” The White House has historically maintained regular access for journalists representing a broad range of news organizations, allowing them to question the President, attend briefings, and document the activities of the executive branch. The press corps also serves a practical function in providing independent accounts of presidential movements and events to the public. CNN, in particular, has been part of the traditional five-network television pool that follows the President and provides ongoing coverage of presidential appearances. Trump has tried before to use access to the White House and presidential events as leverage against news organizations whose coverage he disliked. During his first term, he revoked the White House press credentials of Jim Acosta, then a CNN correspondent, after a contentious exchange during a news conference. CNN challenged the decision in court, and the restriction was overturned. The dispute over access has become part of a broader pattern in Trump’s relationship with the news media. Since returning to office, he has pursued legal action against news organizations including The New York Times, The Wall Street Journal, and the BBC after reports he objected to. The cases against the Times and Journal were subsequently dismissed, while the BBC has sought to have the case dismissed. The Trump Administration has repeatedly called itself the “most transparent Administration in history,” pointing to the President’s frequent gaggles with reporters.

Resident Evil Is a Fleet, Entertaining Zombie-Filled Reboot
2026-09-18

Resident Evil Is a Fleet, Entertaining Zombie-Filled Reboot

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If Zach Cregger’s career tells us anything, it’s that starting out as a comedian might be the best training for becoming a horror filmmaker. Cregger’s 2022 surprise hit (and directorial debut) Barbarian paved the way for an even bigger success, 2025’s Weapons, a well-written, intelligently directed picture featuring sterling performances from Julia Garner, Josh Brolin, and most of all Amy Madigan, who won an Academy Award for her role as Aunt Gladys, an unctuously polite polyester-pantsuit-wearing costume jewelry addict who also happens to be a witch, having lured a town’s schoolkids away in the middle of the night for her own nefarious purposes. So many contemporary horror films lack a sense of humor, about the world or about themselves. But Cregger, a founder of the comedy troupe The Whitest Kids U’Know, gets that it’s an essential ingredient. Weapons, despite its overarching aura of dread (and more than a few mildly traumatizing visuals), didn’t come at its audience like a doomy thundercloud. Instead, it found ways to lure us in, with scattered morsels of terror as well as laughs. And unlike too many modern horror films, it featured a confident, definitive ending that was both mortifying and funny, the exact opposite of the carelessly worked-out wrap-up we so often get. Cregger didn't leave you wondering what the heck you just watched—or, worse, wondering why you’d invested the time. His latest film, Resident Evil, a riff on the enduring and now practically antique Japanese video game, isn’t as clever, funny, or grimly haunting as Weapons—but then, it’s simply a different type of beast altogether. The picture is loose-limbed and rambling by design, a kind of shaggy-dog story that reflects the experience of maneuvering a character, via a controller, through a landscape of obstacles. And while some fans of the game have complained that Cregger has failed to include their favorite characters, he has perhaps wisely decided to focus on one: Austin Abrams, who portrayed the drug addict James in Weapons, plays Bryan Hodukavich, a hapless medical courier charged with transporting a very important parcel to far-away Raccoon City General Hospital, which can be reached only by driving across treacherous, icy mountain terrain. His ramshackle vehicle slips and slides on the slick roads, and his headlights flash on the figure of a wild-eyed, bloodied woman just as he hits her. We know, though he doesn’t seem to, that she’s a zombie lady. Stricken with horror at what he’s just done, he locates the woman—she was hit hard enough to be thrown into the woods nearby—and bundles her into the passenger seat, barely taking note of the fact that she’s shoeless and wearing a sleeveless floral dress in the middle of winter. As he drives, frantically, she repeatedly reaches toward his face with her clawlike fingers, as if to caress him; each time, he fails to notice, and she draws back abruptly, her glassy eyes registering nothing but raw need. It’s a predictable yet somehow hilarious gag, and Andrea Miltner, as the zombie woman, does a lot with a little. The mishaps pile up one by one: a state trooper stops Bryan for speeding, and reluctantly agrees to transport the zombie woman in his own vehicle—a good solution but, as it turns out, only a temporary one. Other stuff happens. Bryan must race across a fenced-in field, pursued by a dog—which is of course not exactly a dog. He’s thrilled to have located a shotgun in what looks to be a deserted farmhouse—he’s just got to find some ammo learn how to load the thing. Later, he’ll encounter a duo of travelers (Paul Walter Hauser and Kali Reis) who inform them they’re representatives of a shadowy group known as the Umbrella Corporation, and they reveal the significance of the package he has been protecting so gingerly. (Previously, he’d been convinced it was a tiny heart destined to save the life of a child.) Amid all this, he worries about a cellphone call he’d received earlier from his girlfriend, informing him of a potentially relationship-changing situation. He’s desperate to get back to her, but he can’t get a signal. And over and over again, he's flummoxed by a single, ancient device: he encounters one padlock after another, but of course, he's never got the key. Naturally, as the story moves forward, the zombies begin piling up in alarming quantities. To describe the creatures in detail would be giving too much away: let’s just say, for now, that there are slimy tentacles involved, and vomit and pustules also make an appearance. Resident Evil is largely a one-man show, and Abrams carries it deftly. In the movie’s early scenes, his eyes have a checked-out quality; he’s like an absentee participant in his own life. But as the action, and the danger, intensifies, he becomes more and more alive. By the end of this movie's fleet 90 minutes, his battle seems unwinnable, and Cregger and his co-write Shay Hatton leave him almost literally hanging, begging for a sequel. The Resident Evil gestalt has been interpreted in movie form before, in the series of pictures made by Paul W.S. Anderson in the early 2000s through 2016; a ten-year gap is certainly a reasonable length of time to warrant a series reboot. In the meantime, although Bryan’s fate is unknowable, Resident Evil leaves you satisfied enough. Time, as we measure it in movies, has already proved that zombies aren’t going anywhere. We need them even more than they need us.

In The History of Concrete, John Wilson Is an Affable Tour Guide Into Unknown Worlds
2026-09-18

In The History of Concrete, John Wilson Is an Affable Tour Guide Into Unknown Worlds

TIME | 2026-09-18

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings John Wilson goes deep in The History of Concrete, but not just on the building material Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If you know anything about filmmaker John Wilson, you’ll intuit that his new documentary The History of Concrete is barely about concrete at all. Earlier this decade, Wilson’s HBO show, How To With John Wilson, provided a sometimes compelling, sometimes aggravating glimpse into the lives of ordinary New Yorkers, as well as into Wilson’s own woolly, discursive mode of thinking. In one episode, Wilson walked around a New York neighborhood until he saw an Italian flag, figuring that the person who’d hung it would know enough about Italian food to help him learn to cook risotto, which he wanted to make as a treat for his elderly, babushka’ed landlady. In another installment, he sought ways to protect his furniture from his beloved yet destructive cat, and in so doing, stumbled upon an anti-circumcision activist, who in turn led him to a musician and inventor who’d developed a foreskin restoration device. (It was called the TLC Tugger.) In Wilson’s world, the phrase “One thing leads to another” takes on heightened meaning. He shifts gears according to who and what he encounters during the filmmaking process, which is how a simple quest to cover a chair can morph into an anxiety-riddled meditation on the possibility that maybe life is better uncut. The History of Concrete is essentially more of the same, though this time, Wilson’s observations are stretched out at a more leisurely pace, rather than pieced together into bite-sized segments. Wilson begins by describing the ennui he felt after his show ended. He used to have a purpose; suddenly, he needed to both seek inspiration and drum up work. He describes unsavory moneymaking offers that have come his way, like doing Depend underwear ads with the Naked Cowboy—an easy No. He laments his lack of direction. In his characteristic wry juxtaposition of filmed images and voiceover, he explains that he’d always been proud of taking “the road less traveled, where no one else would dare to voyage,” over grab-and-go footage of street signs that read “Buttzman Road” and “Titman Road.” He takes a WGA-sponsored workshop on how to write a successful Hallmark romance. Then, while doing home repairs—badly, he admits—he begins musing on the fact that concrete is everywhere. Why not make a documentary about it? Wilson tries to sell his idea to a group of poker-faced potential supporters, each enclosed in a tidy little Zoom square. Somehow, the idea gets off the ground. From there, Wilson explains the basics of this ubiquitous material. For example, he makes the distinction between cement—a component of concrete—and concrete itself, a mix of cement, water, sand, and stone. He travels to Rome, where he films examples of early Roman concrete and also visits a janky wax museum, because why not? He attends a concrete convention, and notes that in order to attend, he had to sign a document promising he would not “paint concrete in a negative light.” He visits a company that preserves the tattoos of loved ones, which also means conserving the skin underneath, which makes the whole thing suitable for framing. You may have no idea what this has to do with concrete, but you’ll be so amazed—and probably appalled—that you’ll forget why it matters. But if Wilson has a great gift, it lies in being able to talk to anyone without mocking them. OK, he may gently mock his subjects a little—but only in an amused way, never a cruel one. The best segments in The History of Concrete involve his encounters with the New Yorkers, or Long Islanders, who are essentially right in his own backyard. There’s the exceedingly good-natured guy who runs an outfit called GumBusters out of Brooklyn: he travels around the city with a special gizmo that “vaporizes” dark splotches of gum that have been ground into the sidewalk. Most affecting of all is the friendship Wilson strikes up with a bar-band musician named Jack, who regularly plays dive bars on Long Island. When Jack’s fiancée dies suddenly, it’s Wilson’s company he seeks. Jack, a somewhat portly past-middle-age rock’n’roller who, by day, hands out whiskey and bourbon samples in liquor stores, is bereft at the loss of his love. Wilson investigates and find out that Jack’s deceased partner was an esteemed judge, the first Asian American judge in New York City. He finds an old picture of the two of them and realizes they’d been a couple for a very long time, musing aloud on how they had truly built a life together, though they may seem like opposites. Again, what does any of this have to do with concrete? Aside from, perhaps, the metaphorical truth that the most enduring relationships are built on a solid foundation of trust and love? The History of Concrete will answer some questions you never thought to ask about that hard, gray substance found in every developed area around the world. But mostly, it will show you how Wilson’s mind works, how it jumps from one seemingly random thought to the next and somehow miraculously connects them. Depending on your tolerance for Wilson’s somewhat rambling narration, as well as his twangy, cartoon-character speaking voice, your mind may sometimes wander off his track. But Wilson is an affable armchair tour guide into offline worlds you’d never have thought to explore, and the movie has a casual, ambling charm. This is killing time in movie form—though it also flirts with the possibility that killing time and spending it may amount to the same thing.

The Greenland Fiasco Stumbles to an End
2026-09-19

The Greenland Fiasco Stumbles to an End

The Atlantic | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Jesper Møller Sørensen, the Danish ambassador to Washington, projected an air of calm. He was serving hors d’oeuvres, including reindeer sausage and smoked salmon, at his residence, where American, Danish, and Greenlandic guests had gathered for a Wednesday event billed as “A Taste of the Arctic.” On the terrace, he told me that months-long discussions about the future of Greenland were heading in a good direction. But, he warned, the outcome would depend on whether one person could be swayed. It was obvious that he was talking about President Trump, who has long coveted Greenland, a semiautonomous territory of Denmark. Trump has claimed that the United States needs the island for national security, at times declining to rule out using force to acquire it. When his rhetoric became more threatening earlier this year, the Danish armed forces flew emergency blood supplies and explosives to the island to prepare for a potential U.S. attack. The Danish prime minister, Mette Frederiksen, warned that a U.S. invasion would mean that “everything ends” for NATO—something of an understatement for a scenario in which one founding member of the alliance attacks another. Read: Denmark’s army chief says he’s ready to defend Greenland At the World Economic Forum in Davos, in January, Trump stepped back from the brink, proclaiming the “framework of a future deal.” In the subsequent months, he returned to the topic in fits of pique, at one point arguing that Greenland “should be controlled by the United States, not Denmark.” Meanwhile, representatives from the United States, Denmark, and Greenland met to consider ways to bolster U.S. security interests on the island and fend off hostile powers in the Arctic region, namely Russia and China. Now the promised “future deal” appears to be here. It builds on a Cold War agreement between the U.S. and Denmark and includes no transfer of territory to the United States. Instead, it envisions the kind of expanded U.S.-military access and multilateral cooperation that leaders from Denmark and Greenland said was available all along, and doesn’t threaten to blow up the security architecture that the U.S. has helped maintain for nearly 80 years. Earlier tonight, Trump wrote on social media that Washington “will FOREVER have the complete ability to do what is necessary in Greenland in order to secure and defend the security of Greenland, and the United States of America.” He also said that U.S. adversaries will be barred from having bases on Greenland or making sensitive investments in the island, which is rich in minerals and other raw materials. A Cold War–era agreement signed by Denmark and the United States already gave Washington broad latitude to conduct military operations in the territory, and the U.S. Space Force currently has a base on the island’s northwest coast. The United States once stationed thousands of soldiers across multiple bases and radar sites, but it shrank its presence in the decades after the Cold War. Current and former U.S. officials told me that one area of concern in Washington was the possibility that Greenland, not party to that 1951 agreement, might vote for independence or otherwise alter its political allegiances, and then proceed to restrict U.S. infrastructure and access. The new agreement will also include Greenland and bind all three parties to a more permanent U.S. presence; the U.S. will, among other things, have the ability to build additional bases and use the island’s airspace. Trump wrote, “We will immediately begin the process of developing a large Military presence in the appropriate part of Greenland, of which there are many.” U.S. and European officials told me that the agreement will affirm the right of Greenland’s population to determine its own future. It is also expected to include language barring non-NATO bases as well as mechanisms for blocking Chinese and Russian investments. Secretary of State Marco Rubio, whose deputies played a leading role in the discussions, hailed the agreement. “This deal permanently and completely addresses our national security concerns in Greenland,” he said in a statement. Danish officials said that the three parties will sign the agreement at the United Nations General Assembly next week and that it will enter into force after necessary parliamentary procedures. Greenland’s premier, Jens-Frederik Nielsen, said that the accord “recognizes Greenland’s interests and our place in the international cooperation.” Frederiksen, the Danish prime minister, said that the agreement is a boon for NATO and Europe as well. The statements were positive but restrained, reflecting the pain inflicted by this saga. Europeans found it difficult to decipher Trump’s motives—or to understand whether they were about to be attacked or simply trolled. One day, there was menacing rhetoric on Truth Social; the next, memes on X of penguins. Read: The hole in Trump’s rationale for acquiring Greenland It was already late at night in Nuuk, Greenland’s capital, when Trump announced the deal. But a reporter from Denmark’s public broadcaster observed politicians going in and out of the government building, reflecting fervent interest in the American president’s designs on their land. Washington may have achieved its security objectives. But it has lost something else: trust among its hosts. “Goodbye, U.S.A.,” one resident said.
